Deciding Subtyping for Asynchronous Multiparty Sessions
Elaine Li, Felix Stutz, Thomas Wies

Abstract
Multiparty session types (MSTs) are a type-based approach to verifying communication protocols, represented as global types in the framework. We present a precise subtyping relation for asynchronous MSTs with communicating state machines (CSMs) as implementation model. We address two problems: when can a local implementation safely substitute another, and when does an arbitrary CSM implement a global type? We define safety with respect to a given global type, in terms of subprotocol fidelity and deadlock freedom. Our implementation model subsumes existing work which considers local types with restricted choice. We exploit the connection between MST subtyping and refinement to formulate concise conditions that are directly checkable on the candidate implementations, and use them to show that both problems are decidable in polynomial time.



1
Introduction

Multiparty session types (MSTs)
[
31
]
are a type-based approach to verifying communication protocols. In MST frameworks, a communication protocol is expressed as a
global type
, which describes the interactions of all protocol participants from a birds-eye view.
The key property of interest in MST frameworks is
implementability
, which asks whether there exists a collection of local implementations, one per protocol participant, that is deadlock-free and produces the same set of behaviors described by the global type.
The latter property is known as
protocol fidelity
.
Given an implementable global type, the
synthesis
problem asks to compute such a collection.
To solve implementability and synthesis, MST frameworks are often equipped with a
projection operator
, which is a partial map from global types to a collection of local implementations.
Projection operators compute a correct implementation for a given global type if one exists.

However, projection operators only compute one candidate out of many possible implementations for a given global type, which narrows the usability of MST frameworks. As we demonstrate below, substituting this candidate can in some cases achieve an exponential reduction in the size of the local implementation.
Furthermore, applications may sometimes require that an implementation produce only a subset of the global type’s specified behaviors.
We refer to this property as
subprotocol fidelity
.
For example, a general client-server protocol may customize the set of requests it handles to the specific devices it runs on. Subtyping reintroduces this flexibility into MST frameworks, by characterizing when an implementation can replace another while preserving desirable correctness guarantees.

Formally, a subtyping relation is a reflexive and transitive relation that respects Liskov and Wing’s substitution principle
[
38
]
:
T^{\prime}
is a subtype of
T
when
T^{\prime}
can be
safely
used in any context that expects a term of type
T
.
While implementability for MSTs was originally defined on syntactic local types
[
29
,
31
]
, other implementation models have since been investigated, including communicating session automata
[
21
]
and behavioral contracts
[
16
]
.
We motivate our work with the observation that a subtyping relation is only as powerful as its notion of safety, and the expressivity of its underlying implementation model.
Existing subtyping relations adopt a notion of safety that is agnostic to a global specification.
For example,
[
2
,
3
]
define safety as the successful completion of a single role in binary sessions,
[
36
]
defines safety as eventual reception and progress of all roles in multiparty sessions, and
[
26
]
defines safety as the termination of all roles in multiparty sessions.
As a result, these subtyping relations eagerly reject subtypes that are viable for the specific global type at hand.
In addition, existing implementation models are restricted to local types with
directed choice
for branching, or equivalent representations thereof
[
9
]
, which prohibit a role from sending messages to or receiving messages from different participants in a choice. This restrictiveness undermines the flexibility that subtyping is fundamentally designed to provide.

We present a subtyping relation that extends prior work along both dimensions.
We define a stronger notion of safety with respect to a given global type: a substitution is safe if in all
well-behaved
contexts, the resulting implementation satisfies both deadlock freedom and subprotocol fidelity.
We assume an implementation model of unrestricted communicating state machines (CSMs)
[
4
]
communicating via FIFO channels, which subsumes implementation models in prior work
[
36
,
26
,
20
]
.
We demonstrate that this generalization renders existing subtyping relations which are precise for a restrictive implementation model incomplete.
As a result of both extensions, our subtyping relation requires reasoning about available messages
[
39
]
for completeness, a novel feature that is absent from existing subtyping relations.

Our result applies to global types with
sender-driven
choice, which generalize global types from their original formulation with directed choice
[
31
]
, and borrows insights from recent work on a sound and complete projection operator for this class of global types
[
37
]
.

Contributions.

In this paper, we present the first precise subtyping relation that guarantees deadlock freedom and subprotocol fidelity with respect to a global type, and that assumes an unrestricted, asynchronous CSM implementation model.
We solve the
Protocol Verification
problem and the
Protocol Refinement
problem with respect to global type
\mathbf{G}
and a set of roles
\mathcal{P}
:

1.

Protocol Verification
: Given a CSM
\mathcal{A}
, does
\mathcal{A}
implement
\mathbf{G}
?

2.

Protocol Refinement
: Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a role and let
B
be a safe implementation for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in any well-behaved context for
\mathbf{G}
. Given
A
, can
A
safely replace
B
in any well-behaved context for
\mathbf{G}
?

We exploit the connection between MST subtyping and CSM refinement to formulate concise conditions that are directly checkable on candidate state machines. Using this characterization, we show that both problems are decidable in polynomial time.

2
Motivation

We first showcase that sound and complete projection operators can yield local implementations that are exponential in the size of its global type, but can be reduced to constant size by subtyping.
We then demonstrate the restrictiveness of existing subtyping relations both in terms of their notion of safety and their implementation model.

Subset projection with exponentially many states.

We first construct a family of implementable global types
\mathbf{G}_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{G}_{n}
has size linear in
n
and the deterministic finite state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
that recognizes
the projection of the global language onto
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s alphabet
\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, denoted
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}_{n}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
, has size exponential in
n
.

The construction of the
\mathbf{G}_{n}
’s builds on the regular expression
(a^{*}(ab^{*})^{n}a)^{*}
, which can only be recognized by a deterministic finite state machine that grows exponentially with
n

[
23
, Thm. 11]
.

First, we construct the part for
(ab^{*})^{i}a
recursively.
In global types,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{\to}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{:}\kern 1.19995ptm
denotes role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
sending a message
m
to role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,
+
denotes choice,
\mu t
binds a recursion variable
t
that can be used in the continuation, and
0
denotes termination.

\small G_{i}\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pta.\,μt_{3,i}.+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994ptm_{3}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994ptb.\,t_{3,i}\,\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994ptn_{3}.\,G_{i-1}\end{cases}\;\text{for }i>0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ and }\hskip 9.24994ptG_{0}\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pta.\,t_{1}

Here, each
G_{i}
for
i>0
generates
(ab^{*})
and
G_{0}
adds the last
a
.
Role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s choice to send either
m_{3}
or
n_{3}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
respectively encodes the choice to continue iterating
b
’s or to stop in
b^{*}
;
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
however, is not involved in this exchange and thus
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s local language is isomorphic to
(ab^{*})^{i}a
.

Next, we define some scaffolding
G(\hbox{-})
for the outermost Kleene Star and the first
a^{*}
:

\small G(G^{\prime})\coloneq μt_{1}.\,+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994ptm_{1}.\,μt_{2}.+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994ptm_{2}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pta.\,t_{2}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994ptn_{2}.\,G^{\prime}\end{cases}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994ptn_{1}.\,0\end{cases}\kern 4.62497pt.

We combine both to obtain the family

\mathbf{G}_{n}\coloneq G(G_{n})
.

As
\mathbf{G}_{n}
is implementable, the subset projection
[
37
]
for each role is defined.
One feature of the implementations computed by this projection operator is
local language preservation
, meaning that the language recognized by the local implementation is precisely the projection of the global language onto its alphabet, e.g.
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}_{n}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}

for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
with alphabet
\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
In this case,
because
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}_{n}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
can only be recognized by a deterministic finite state machine with size exponential in
n
, the corresponding local language preserving implementation also has size exponential in
n
.

However, not all implementations need to satisfy local language preservation.
Consider the type
\mu t.({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{\to}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{:}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,t+{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{\to}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{:}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0)
. The projection of the global language onto
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
limits
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
to only receiving a sequence of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
messages terminated by a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
message.
However, an implementation for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
can rely on
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to send correct sequences of messages, and instead accept any message that it receives.
A similar pattern arises in the family
\mathbf{G}_{n}
, where the exponentially-sized implementation for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
can simply be substituted with an automaton that allows to receive any message from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

The restrictiveness of existing MST subtyping relations.

Consider the two implementations for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, represented as finite state machines
A
and
B
in
Figs.

1(b)
and
1(a)
.
State machine
A
embodies the idea of input covariance
[
25
]
by adding receive actions, namely

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm
, which denotes role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
receiving a message
m
from role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
But is it the case that
A
is a subtype of
B
?
A preliminary answer based on prior work
[
34
,
26
]
is
no
, for the reason that
A
falls outside of the implementation models considered in these works: the initial state in
A
contains outgoing receive transitions from two distinct senders,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
, and one of the final states contains an outgoing transition. Thus, there exists no local type representation of
A
.

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm

(a)

A

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm

(b)

B

Figure 1
:
Two state machines for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}

As a first step, let us generalize the implementation model to machines with arbitrary finite state control, and revisit the question. It turns out that the answer now depends on what protocol role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, alongside the other roles in the context, is following.
Consider the two global types

\small\mathbf{G}_{1}\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994ptm.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994ptm.\,0\hskip 18.49988pt\text{and}\hskip 18.49988pt\mathbf{G}_{2}\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994ptm.\,0\,\kern 4.62497pt.

We observe that
A
is a subtype of
B
under the context of
\mathbf{G}_{2}
, but not under the context of
\mathbf{G}_{1}
.
Suppose that roles
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
are both following
\mathbf{G}_{1}
, and thus both roles send a message
m
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
. Under asynchrony, the two messages can arrive in
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s channel in any order; this holds even in a synchronous setting.
Therefore, there exists an execution trace in which
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
takes the transition labeled

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm
in
A
and first receives from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
. Role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
then finds itself in a final state with a pending message from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
that it is unable to receive, thus causing a deadlock in the CSM.
On the other hand, if
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
were following
\mathbf{G}_{2}
, the addition of the receive transition

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm
is safe because it is never enabled, and thus
A
can safely compose with any context following
\mathbf{G}_{2}
without violating protocol fidelity and deadlock freedom.

3
Preliminaries

We restate relevant definitions from
[
37
]
.

Words.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite alphabet.

\Sigma^{*}
denotes the set of finite words over
\Sigma
,
\Sigma^{\omega}
the set of infinite words, and
\Sigma^{\infty}\negthinspace
their union
\Sigma^{*}\cup\Sigma^{\omega}
.
A word
u\in\Sigma^{*}
is a
prefix
of word
v\in\Sigma^{\infty}
, denoted
u\leq v
, if there exists
w\in\Sigma^{\infty}
with
u\cdot w=v
.

Message Alphabet.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a set of roles and
\mathcal{V}
be a set of messages. We define the set of
synchronous events

Σ_{\mathit{sync}}\coloneq\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{\to}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{:}\kern 1.19995ptm\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}∈\mathcal{P}\text{ and }m∈\mathcal{V}\}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{\to}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{:}\kern 1.19995ptm
denotes that message
m
is sent by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
atomically.
This is split for
asynchronous events
.
For a role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, we define the alphabet

\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P},\;m\in\mathcal{V}\}
of
send
events
and the alphabet

\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},?}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P},\;m\in\mathcal{V}\}
of
receive
events.
The event
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm
denotes role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
sending a message
m
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm
denotes role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
receiving a message
m
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
We write
\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}\cup\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},?}
,

\Sigma_{!}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}
, and

\Sigma_{?}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},?}
.
Finally,
Σ_{\mathit{async}}=\Sigma_{!}\cup\Sigma_{?}
.
We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is
active
in
x\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}
if
x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
For each role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, we define a homomorphism
{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
, where
x{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=x
if
x\in\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
\varepsilon
otherwise.
We fix
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{V}
in the rest of the paper.

Global Types – Syntax.

Global types for MSTs
[
39
]
are defined by the grammar:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\Coloneqq 0\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{\to}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 1.19995pt{:}\kern 1.19995ptm_{i}.G_{i}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\mu t.\;G\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptt

where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}
range over
\mathcal{P}
,
m_{i}
over
\mathcal{V}
, and
t
over a set of recursion variables.

We require each branch of a choice to be distinct:

∀i,j∈I.\,i≠j⇒({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i},m_{i})≠({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j},m_{j})
,
the sender and receiver of an event to be distinct:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}
for each
i\in I
,
and recursion to be guarded:
in
μt.\,G
, there is at least one message between
μt
and each
t
in
G
.
We omit
\sum
for singleton choices.
When working with a protocol described by a global type, we use
\mathbf{G}
to refer to the top-level type, and
G
to refer to its subterms.

We use the extended definition of global types from
[
39
]
featuring
sender-driven choice
.
This definition subsumes classical MSTs that only allow
directed choice

[
31
]
.
We focus on communication primitives and omit features like delegation or parametrization, and refer the reader to
Section

7
for a discussion of different MST frameworks.

Global Types – Semantics.

As a basis for the semantics of a global type
\mathbf{G}
, we construct a finite state machine

\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})=(Q_{\mathbf{G}},Σ_{\mathit{sync}},δ_{\mathbf{G}},q_{0,\mathbf{G}},F_{\mathbf{G}})

where

•

Q_{\mathbf{G}}
is the set of all syntactic subterms in
\mathbf{G}
together with the term
0
,

•

δ_{\mathbf{G}}
consists of the transitions
(\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{\to}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 1.19995pt{:}\kern 1.19995ptm_{i}.G_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{\to}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 1.19995pt{:}\kern 1.19995ptm_{i},G_{i})
for each
i∈I
,
as well as
(μt.G^{\prime},ε,G^{\prime})
and
(t,ε,μt.G^{\prime})
for each subterm
\mu t.G^{\prime}
,

•

q_{0,\mathbf{G}}=\mathbf{G}
and

F_{\mathbf{G}}=\{0\}
.

We define a homomorphism

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}
onto the asynchronous alphabet:

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{\to}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{:}\kern 1.19995ptm)\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm\kern 5.0pt.

The semantics
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
of a global type
\mathbf{G}
is given by

\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}))))
where
\mathcal{C}^{\sim}
is the closure under the indistinguishability relation
\sim

[
39
]
.
Two events are independent if they are not related by the
happened-before
relation
[
33
]
. For instance, any two send events from distinct senders are independent.
Two words are indistinguishable if one can be reordered into the other by repeatedly swapping consecutive independent events. The full definition can be found in
Section

0.A.1
.

We call a state
q_{G}\in Q_{\mathbf{G}}
a
send-originating
state, denoted
q_{G}\in Q_{\mathbf{G},!}
for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
if there exists a transition
q_{G}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.98329pt{\to}\kern 0.98329pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.98329pt{:}\kern 0.98329ptm}q_{G^{\prime}}\in\delta_{\mathbf{G}}
, and a
receive-originating
state, denoted
q_{G}\in Q_{\mathbf{G},?}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
if there exists a transition
q_{G}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.98329pt{\to}\kern 0.98329pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.98329pt{:}\kern 0.98329ptm}q_{G^{\prime}}\in\delta_{\mathbf{G}}
.
We omit mention of role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
when clear from context.

Communicating State Machine
[
4
]
.

\mathcal{A}=\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is a CSM over
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{V}
if

{A}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=(Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},q_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},F_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})

is a deterministic finite state machine
over
\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.
Let

\prod_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

denote the set of global states and

\mathsf{Chan}=\{({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\}

denote the set of channels.
A
configuration
of
\mathcal{A}
is a pair
(\vec{s},\xi)
, where
\vec{s}\,
is a global state and

\xi:\mathsf{Chan}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}

is a mapping from each channel to a sequence of messages.
We use
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to denote the state of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
\vec{s}
.
The CSM transition relation, denoted
\rightarrow
, is defined as follows.

•

(\vec{s},\xi)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556ptm}(\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
if

(\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm,\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}=\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
for every role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

\xi^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})=\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\cdot m
and
\xi^{\prime}(c)=\xi(c)
for every other channel
c\in\mathsf{Chan}
.

•

(\vec{s},\xi)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556ptm}(\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
if

(\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm,\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,

\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}=\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
for every role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,

\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})=m\cdot\xi^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})

and
\xi^{\prime}(c)=\xi(c)
for every other channel
c\in\mathsf{Chan}
.

In the initial configuration
(\vec{s}_{0},\xi_{0})
, each role’s state in
\vec{s}_{0}
is the initial state
q_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
of
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and
\xi_{0}
maps each channel to
\varepsilon
.
A configuration
(\vec{s},\xi)
is said to be
final
iff
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is final for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
\xi
maps each channel to
\varepsilon
.
Runs and traces are defined in the expected way.
A run is
maximal
if either it is finite and ends in a final configuration, or it is infinite.
The language
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
of the CSM
\mathcal{A}
is defined as the set of maximal traces.
A configuration
(\vec{s},\xi)
is a
deadlock
if it is not final and has no outgoing transitions.
A CSM is
deadlock-free
if no reachable configuration is a deadlock.

Definition 1
(Implementability)

We say that a CSM
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
implements a global type
\mathbf{G}
if the following two properties hold:

(i)

protocol fidelity:
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
, and

(ii)

deadlock freedom:
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock-free.

A global type
\mathbf{G}
is
implementable
if there exists a CSM that implements it.

One candidate implementation for global types can be computed directly from
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
, by removing actions unrelated to each role and determinizing the result.
The following two definitions define this candidate implementation in two steps.

Definition 2
(Projection by Erasure
[
37
]
)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be some global type
with its state machine

\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})=(Q_{\mathbf{G}},Σ_{\mathit{sync}},\delta_{\mathbf{G}},q_{0,\mathbf{G}},F_{\mathbf{G}})
.
For each role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, we define the state machine

\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\,=(Q_{\mathbf{G}},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\},\delta_{\downarrow},q_{0,\mathbf{G}},F_{\mathbf{G}})

where

\delta_{\downarrow}\coloneq\{q\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(a){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}q^{\prime}\mid q\xrightarrow{a}q^{\prime}\in\delta_{\mathbf{G}}\}
.
By definition of
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\hbox{-})
, it holds that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(a){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\}
.

We determinize
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
via a standard subset construction
[
45
]
to obtain a deterministic local state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Note that the construction ensures that
Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
only contains subsets of
Q_{\mathbf{G}}
whose states are reachable via the same traces.

Definition 3
(Subset Construction
[
37
]
)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a role.
Then, the
subset construction
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is defined as

\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})=\bigl(Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},F_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\bigr)\text{ where }

•

\delta(s,a)\coloneq\{q^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathbf{G}}\mid\exists q\in s,q\xrightarrow{a}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}q^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}\},

for
every

s\subseteq Q_{\mathbf{G}}
and

a\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
,

•

s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq\{q\in Q_{\mathbf{G}}\mid q_{0,\mathbf{G}}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}q\in\delta_{\downarrow}\}
,

•

Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq\mathrm{lfp}_{\{s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}}^{\subseteq}\lambda Q.\,Q\cup\{\delta(s,a)\mid s\in Q\land a\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}\setminus\{\emptyset\}
,

•

\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq{\delta}|_{Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\times\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
, and

•

F_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq\{s\in Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mid s\cap F_{\mathbf{G}}\neq\emptyset\}
.

Li et al.
[
37
]
showed that if
\mathbf{G}
is implementable, then
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
implements
\mathbf{G}
and satisfies the following property:

Definition 4

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type.
We call an implementation
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

local language preserving
with respect to
\mathbf{G}
if
\mathcal{L}(A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.

For the remainder of the paper, we fix a global type
\mathbf{G}
that we assume is implementable.

4
Deciding
Protocol Verification

Protocol Verification
asks: Given a CSM
\mathcal{A}
, does
\mathcal{A}
implement
\mathbf{G}
?
For two CSMs
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, we say that
\mathcal{A}
refines
\mathcal{B}
if and only if every trace in
\mathcal{A}
is a trace in
\mathcal{B}
, and a trace in
\mathcal{A}
terminates maximally in
\mathcal{A}
if and only if it terminates maximally in
\mathcal{B}
. If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
refine each other, we say that they are equivalent.
Further, in the case that
\mathcal{B}
is deadlock-free, one can simplify the condition to the following: every trace in
\mathcal{A}
is a trace in
\mathcal{B}
, and if a trace terminates in
\mathcal{A}
, then it terminates in
\mathcal{B}
and is maximal in
\mathcal{A}
.

We can recast
Protocol Verification
in terms of CSM refinement using the fact that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is an implementation for
\mathbf{G}
.
Therefore, the question amounts to asking whether
\mathcal{A}
and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
are equivalent.

Our goal is then to present a characterization
C_{1}
that satisfies the following:

Theorem 4.1

Let
\mathbf{G}
be an implementable global type and
\mathcal{A}
be a CSM.
Then,
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
and
\mathcal{A}
are equivalent if and only if
C_{1}
is satisfied.

We motivate our characterization for
Protocol Verification
using a series of examples.
Consider the following simple global type
\mathbf{G}_{1}
:

\small\mathbf{G}_{1}\coloneq+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0\,\\

Figure 2
:
Subset construction of
\mathbf{G}_{1}
onto
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and three alternative implementations

For a local state machine in a CSM, control flow is determined by both the local transition relation and the global channel state.
However, in some cases, the local information is redundant: the role’s channel contents alone are enough to enforce that it produces the correct behaviors.
In the example above, after
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
chooses to send
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
either
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
will guarantee that the correct message, i.e. the same one, is sent back to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s state machine can rely on its channel contents to follow the protocol – it does not need separate control states for each message.
In fact, we can further replace
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s control states after sending with an accepting universal receive state, as shown in
A_{2}
in
Fig.

2(c)
.
Finally, we can add send transitions from unreachable states, as shown in
A_{3}
in
Fig.

2(d)
.

\mathbf{G}_{2}
onto
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and two alternative implementations

Similar patterns arise for send actions.
Consider the following variation of the first global type,
\mathbf{G}_{2}
:

\small\mathbf{G}_{2}\coloneq+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0\,\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,0\,\end{cases}

The subset construction from
[
37
]
yields the state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
shown in
Fig.

3(a)
.

Our reasoning above shows that
A_{4}
, depicted in
Fig.

3(b)
, is a correct alternative implementation for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Now observe that the pre-states of the two
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
transitions can be collapsed because their continuations are identical. This yields another correct alternative implementation
A_{5}
, shown in
Fig.

3(c)
.

Informally, the subset construction takes a “maximalist” approach, creating as many distinct states as possible from the global type, and checking whether they are enough to guarantee that the role behaves correctly.
However, sometimes this maximalism creates redundancy: just because two states are distinct according to the global type does not mean they need to be. In these cases, an implementation has the flexibility to merge certain distinct states together, or add transitions to a state.
We wish to precisely characterize when such modifications to local state machines preserve protocol fidelity and deadlock freedom.

Our conditions for
C_{1}
are inspired by the Send and Receive Validity conditions that precisely characterize implementability for global types, given in
[
37
]
.
We restate the conditions, in addition to relevant definitions, for clarity.

Definition 5
(Available messages
[
39
]
)

The set of available messages is recursively defined on the structure of the global type.
For completeness, we need to unfold the distinct recursion variables once.
For this, we define a map
\mathit{get\mu}
from variable to subterms and write
\mathit{get\mu}_{\mathbf{G}}
for
\mathit{get\mu}(\mathbf{G})
:

\mathit{get\mu}(0)\coloneq[\,]

\mathit{get\mu}(t)\coloneq[\,]

\mathit{get\mu}(μt.G)\coloneq[t\mapsto G]∪\mathit{get\mu}(G)

\mathit{get\mu}(\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.01996pt{\to}\kern 1.01996pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 1.01996pt{:}\kern 1.01996ptm_{i}.G_{i})\coloneq\bigcup_{i∈I}\mathit{get\mu}(G_{i})

The function
M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(\hbox{-}\ldots)}
keeps a set of unfolded variables
T
, which is empty initially.

M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(0\ldots)}\coloneq∅\hfill M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(μt.G\ldots)}\coloneq M^{\mathcal{B},T∪\{t\}}_{(G\ldots)}\hfill M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(t\ldots)}\coloneq\begin{cases}∅&\text{if}~t∈T\\
M^{\mathcal{B},T∪\{t\}}_{(\mathit{get\mu}_{\mathbf{G}}(t)\ldots)}&\text{if}~t∉T\end{cases}\\
M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.96594pt{\to}\kern 0.96594pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.96594pt{:}\kern 0.96594ptm_{i}.G_{i}\ldots)}\coloneq\begin{cases}\bigcup_{i∈I,m∈\mathcal{V}}(M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(G_{i}\ldots)}\setminus\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.20253pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.74pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.74pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.74ptm\})∪\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.20253pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.74pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.74pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.74ptm_{i}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\hfill\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}∉\mathcal{B}\\
\bigcup_{i∈I}M^{\mathcal{B}∪\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\},T}_{(G_{i}\ldots)}\hskip 9.24994pt\hfill\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}∈\mathcal{B}\end{cases}

We write
M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}
for
M^{\mathcal{B},\emptyset}_{(G\ldots)}
.
If
\mathcal{B}
is a singleton set, we omit set notation and write
M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G\ldots)}

for
M^{\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}}_{(G\ldots)}
.

Intuitively, the available messages definition captures all of the messages that can be at the head of their respective channels when a particular role is blocked from taking further transitions.

For notational convenience, we define the
origin
and
destination
of a transition following
[
37
]
, but generalized from the subset construction automaton.

Definition 6
(Transition Origin and Destination)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type and let
\delta_{\downarrow}
be the transition relation of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
For
x\in\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
s,s^{\prime}\subseteq Q_{\mathbf{G}}
,
we define the set of
transition origins

\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})
and
transition destinations

\operatorname{tr-dest}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})
as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\{G\in s\mid\exists G^{\prime}\in s^{\prime}.\,G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}\}\;\text{ and }

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr-dest}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\{G^{\prime}\in s^{\prime}\mid\exists G\in s.\,G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Li et al.
[
37
]
showed that
\mathbf{G}
is implementable if and only if the subset construction CSM
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
satisfies Send and Receive Validity for each
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.

Definition 7
(Send Validity)

\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
satisfies
Send Validity
iff every send transition
s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is enabled in all states contained in
s
:

\forall s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}.~x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}\implies\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})=s\kern 5.0pt.

Definition 8
(Receive Validity)

\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
satisfies
Receive Validity
iff no receive transition is enabled in an alternative continuation that originates from the same source state:

\begin{array}[]{l}\forall s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556ptm_{1}}s_{1},\,s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556ptm_{2}}s_{2}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}.\\
\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}\;\implies\;\forall~G_{2}\in\operatorname{tr-dest}(s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556ptm_{2}}s_{2}).\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm_{1}\notin M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G_{2}\ldots)}\kern 5.0pt.\end{array}

We wish to adapt these conditions to define
C_{1}
.
However, unlike Send and Receive Validity, which are defined on special state machines, namely the subset construction for each role, the
Protocol Verification
problem asks whether arbitrary state machines implement the given
\mathbf{G}
.

We first present a
state decoration
function which maps local states in an arbitrary deterministic finite state machine to sets of global states in
\mathbf{G}
.
Intuitively, state decoration captures all global states that can be reached in the projection by erasure automaton
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
on the same prefixes that reach the present state in the local state machine.

Definition 9
(State decoration with respect to
\mathbf{G}
)

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
be a role and let
A=(Q,\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},s_{0},\delta,F)
be a deterministic finite state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Let

\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\,=(Q_{\mathbf{G}},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\},\delta_{\downarrow},q_{0,\mathbf{G}},F_{\mathbf{G}})

be
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s projection by erasure state machine for
\mathbf{G}
.
We define a total function
d_{\mathbf{G},A}:Q\rightarrow 2^{Q_{\mathbf{G}}}
that maps each state in
A
to a subset of states in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
such that:

d_{\mathbf{G},A,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(s)=\{q\in Q_{\mathbf{G}}\mid\exists u\in\Sigma^{*}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}.~s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s\in\delta\land q_{0,\mathbf{G}}\xrightarrow{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}q\in\delta_{\downarrow}\}\kern 5.0pt.

We refer to
d_{\mathbf{G},A,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(s)
as the
decoration set
of
s
, and omit the subscripts
\mathbf{G},A,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
when clear from context.

Remark 1

Note that the subset construction can be viewed as a special state machine for which the state decoration function is the identity function.
In other words, for all
s\in Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
where
Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is the set of states of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
,
d(s)=s
.

We are now equipped to present
C_{1}
.

Definition 10
(
C_{1}
)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type and
\mathcal{A}
be a CSM.

C_{1}
is satisfied when for all
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, with
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=(Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},F_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
denoting the state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
\mathcal{A}
, the following conditions hold:

•

Send Decoration Validity
: every send transition
s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is enabled in all states decorating
s
:

\forall s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556ptm}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}.~\operatorname{tr-orig}(d(s)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556ptm}d(s^{\prime}))=d(s)
.

•

Receive Decoration Validity
: no receive transition is enabled in an alternative continuation originating from the same state:

\begin{array}[]{l}\forall s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556ptm_{1}}s_{1},~s\xrightarrow{x}s_{2}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}.~x\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt\_\implies\\
\qquad\forall G^{\prime}\in\operatorname{tr-dest}(d(s)\xrightarrow{x}d(s_{2})).\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm_{1}\notin M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G^{\prime}\ldots)}.\end{array}

•

Transition Exhaustivity
: every transition that is enabled in some global state decorating
s
must be an outgoing transition from
s
:

\forall s\in Q.~\forall G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}.~G\in d(s)\implies\exists s^{\prime}\in Q.~s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

•

Final State Validity
: a reachable state with a non-empty decorating set is final if its decorating set contains a final global state:

\forall s\in Q.~d(s)\neq\emptyset\implies(d(s)\cap F_{\mathbf{G}}\neq\emptyset\implies s\in F_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.

We want to show the following equivalence to prove
Theorem

4.1
:

C_{1}

\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{A}
refines
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines
\mathcal{A}
.

We address soundness (the forward direction) and completeness (the backward direction) in turn.
Soundness states that
C_{1}
is sufficient to show that
\mathcal{A}
preserves all behaviors of the subset construction, and does not introduce new behaviors.

We say that a state machine
A
for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
satisfies
Local Language Inclusion
if it satisfies
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(A)
.
The following lemma, proven in
Section

0.A.2
, establishes that
Local Language Inclusion
follows from
Transition Exhaustivity
and
Final State Validity
.

Lemma 1

Let
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=(Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},F_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
denote the state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
\mathcal{A}
.
Then,
Transition Exhaustivity
and
Final State Validity
imply

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.

The fact that
\mathcal{A}
preserves behaviors follows immediately from
Local Language Inclusion
.
The fact that
\mathcal{A}
does not introduce new behaviors, on the other hand, is enforced by
Send Decoration Validity
and
Receive Decoration Validity
.

In the soundness proof for each of our conditions, we prove refinement via structural induction on traces.
We show refinement in two steps, first showing that any trace in one CSM is a trace in the other, and then showing that any terminated trace in one CSM is terminated in the other and maximal.

We recall two definitions from
[
37
]
used in the soundness proof.

Definition 11
(Intersection sets)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type and
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
be the corresponding state machine.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a role and

w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
be a word.
We define the set of possible runs
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)

as all maximal runs of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
that are consistent with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s local view of
w
:

\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)\coloneq\{\rho\text{ is a maximal run of }\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\mid w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\leq\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\rho)){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}\kern 5.0pt.

We denote the intersection of the possible run sets for all roles as

I(w)\coloneq\bigcap_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)\kern 5.0pt.

Definition 12
(Unique splitting of a possible run)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
a role, and
w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
a word. Let
\rho
be a possible run in
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)
.
We define the longest prefix of
\rho
matching
w
:

\alpha^{\prime}\coloneq\max\{\rho^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}\leq\rho~\wedge~\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\rho^{\prime})){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\leq w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}\kern 5.0pt.

If
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho
, we can split
\rho
into

\rho=\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta

where
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha\cdot G
,
G^{\prime}
denotes the state following
G
, and
\beta
denotes the suffix of
\rho
following
\alpha\cdot G\cdot G^{\prime}
.
We call
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
the unique splitting of
\rho
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
matching
w
.
We omit the role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
when obvious from context.
This splitting is always unique because the maximal prefix of any
\rho\in\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)
matching
w
is unique.

Lemma 2
(Soundness of
C_{1}
)

C_{1}
implies that
\mathcal{A}
and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
are equivalent.

Proof

The proof that
C_{1}
implies
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines
\mathcal{A}
depends only on
Local Language Inclusion
and can be straightforwardly adapted from
[
37
, Lemma 4.4]
.
We instead focus on showing that
C_{1}
implies
\mathcal{A}
refines
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, which depends on the other two conditions in
C_{1}
.
First, we prove that any trace in
\mathcal{A}
is a trace in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
:

Claim 1:

\forall~w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{\infty}.~w
is a trace in
\mathcal{A}
implies
w
is a trace in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

We prove the claim by induction for all finite
w
.
The infinite case follows from the finite case because
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deterministic and all prefixes of
w
are traces of
\mathcal{A}
and, hence, of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
The base cases, where
w=\varepsilon
, is trivially discharged by the fact that
\varepsilon
is a trace of all CSMs.
In the inductive step, assume that
w
is a trace of
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
x\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}
such that
wx
is a trace of
\mathcal{A}
.
We want to show that
wx
is also a trace of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

From the induction hypothesis, we know that
w
is a trace of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
Let
\xi
be the channel configuration uniquely determined by
w
.
Let
(\vec{s},\xi)
be the
\mathcal{A}
configuration reached on
w
, and let
(\vec{t},\xi)
be the
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
configuration reached on
w
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
be the role such that
x\in\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, and let
s
,
t
denote
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
from the respective CSM configurations reached on
w
for
\mathcal{A}
and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

To show that
wx
is a trace of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, it thus suffices to show that there exists a state
t^{\prime}
and a transition
t\xrightarrow{x}t^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
.

Since
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
implements
\mathbf{G}
, all finite traces of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
are prefixes of
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
.
In other words,
w\in\text{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
.
Let
\rho
be a run such that
\rho\in I(w)
; such a run must exist from
[
37
, Lemma 6.3]
.
Let
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
be the unique splitting of
\rho
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
matching
w
.
From the definition of state decoration, it holds that
G\in d(s)
.
From the definition of the subset construction, it holds that
G\in t
.

We proceed by case analysis on whether
x
is a send or receive event.

•

Case
x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},!}
.
Let
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm
.
By assumption, there exists
s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556ptm}s^{\prime}
in
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
We instantiate
Send Decoration Validity
from
C_{1}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and this transition to obtain:

\operatorname{tr-orig}(d(s)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556ptm}d(s^{\prime}))=d(s)\kern 5.0pt.

From
G\in d(s)
, it follows that there exists
G^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathbf{G}}
such that
G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}
.
Because
G\in t
, the existence of
t^{\prime}
such that
t\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556ptm}t^{\prime}
is a transition in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
follows immediately from the definition of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
’s transition relation.

•

Case
x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},?}
.
Let
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm
.

From the fact that
\rho
is a maximal run in
\mathbf{G}
with unique splitting
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
matching w, it holds that
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}\cdot\,\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}\in\text{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
.
From
[
37
, Lemma 4.3]
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}=\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}))
.
Therefore, there exists a
t^{\prime\prime}
such that
t\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}t^{\prime\prime}
is a transition in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
.
From
Transition Exhaustivity
, there likewise exists an
s^{\prime\prime}
such that
s\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}s^{\prime\prime}
is a transition in
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.

We now proceed by showing that it must be the case that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}=x
.
The reasoning closely follows that in
[
37
, Lemma 6.4]
, which showed that if Receive Validity holds for the subset construction, and some role’s subset construction automaton can perform a receive action, then the trace extended with the receive action remains consistent with any global run it was consistent with before.
We generalize this property in terms of available message sets in the following lemma, whose proof can be found in
Section

0.A.2
.

Lemma 3

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a CSM,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
be a role, and
w
,
wx
be traces of
\mathcal{A}
such that
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm
.
Let
s
be the state of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s state machine in the
\mathcal{A}
configuration reached on
w
.
Let
\rho
be a run that is consistent with
w
, i.e. for all
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}.~w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\leq\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\rho)){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.
Let
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
be the unique splitting of
\rho
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
matching
w
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm\notin M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}_{(G^{\prime}\ldots)}
, then

x=\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
.

We wish to apply
Lemma

3
with
\rho
to conclude that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}=x
.
We satisfy the assumption that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm\notin M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}_{(G^{\prime}\ldots)}
by instantiating
Receive Decoration Validity
with
s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556ptm}s^{\prime}
,
s\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}s^{\prime\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
.
The fact that
G^{\prime}\in\operatorname{tr-dest}(d(s)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}d(s^{\prime\prime}))
follows from the fact that
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
is a run in
\mathbf{G}
and the definition of state decoration (
Definition

9
).
Thus, we conclude from
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}=x
that there exists a transition
t\xrightarrow{x}t^{\prime\prime}
in
