\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
.

This concludes our proof that any trace in
\mathcal{A}
is also a trace of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

Claim 2:

\forall~w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
.
w
is terminated in
\mathcal{A}\implies w
is terminated in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
and
w
is maximal in
\mathcal{A}
.

Let
w
be a terminated trace in
\mathcal{A}
.
By Claim 1,
w
is also a trace in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
Let
\xi
be the channel configuration uniquely determined by
w
.
Let the
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
configuration reached on
w
be
(\vec{t},\xi)
, and let
(\vec{s},\xi)
be the
\mathcal{A}
configuration reached on
w
.
To see that every terminated trace in
\mathcal{A}
is also terminated in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, assume by contradiction that
w
is not terminated in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
Because
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock-free, there must exist a role that can take a step in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
be this role, and let
x
be the transition that is enabled from
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
From
Local Language Inclusion
and the fact that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock-free, it holds that
x
is also enabled from
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
We arrive at a contradiction.
To see that every terminated trace in
\mathcal{A}
in maximal, from the above we know that
w
is terminated in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
. From the fact that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock-free,
w
is maximal in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
: all states in
\vec{t}
are final and all channels in
\xi
are empty.
From
Local Language Inclusion
, it follows that all states in
\vec{s}
are also final, and thus
w
is maximal in
\mathcal{A}
.

∎

Lemma 4
(Completeness of
C_{1}
)

If
\mathcal{A}
and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
are equivalent, then
C_{1}
holds.

We show completeness via modus tollens: we assume a violation in
C_{1}
and the fact that
\mathcal{A}
and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
are equivalent, and prove a contradiction.
Since
C_{1}
is a conjunction of four conditions, we derive a contradiction from the violation of each condition in turn.
In the interest of proof reuse, we specify which of the two refinement conjuncts we contradict for each condition, and refer the reader to
Section

0.A.2
for the full proofs.

From the negation of
Transition Exhaustivity
and
Final State Validity
, we contradict the fact that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines
\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 5

If
\mathcal{A}
violates
Transition Exhaustivity
or
Final State Validity
, then it does not hold that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines
\mathcal{A}
.

Unlike the proofs for
Transition Exhaustivity
and
Final State Validity
, the proofs for the remaining two conditions require both refinement conjuncts to prove a contradiction.
Both proofs find a contradiction by obtaining a witness from the violation of
Send Decoration Validity
and
Receive Decoration Validity
respectively, and showing that the same witness can be used to refute Send and Receive Validity for the subset construction.

Lemma 6

If
\mathcal{A}
violates
Send Decoration Validity
or
Receive Decoration Validity
, then it does not hold that
\mathcal{A}
and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
are equivalent.

5
Deciding
Protocol Refinement

We now turn our attention to
Protocol Refinement
, which asks when an implementation can safely substitute another in all well-behaved contexts with respect to
\mathbf{G}
.
Here, we introduce a new notion of refinement with respect to a global type.

Definition 13
(Protocol refinement with respect to
\mathbf{G}
)

We say that a CSM
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines a CSM
\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
with respect to a global type
\mathbf{G}
if the following properties hold:

(i)

subprotocol fidelity:
\exists S\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})).~\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})=\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(S))
,

(ii)

language inclusion:
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
, and

(iii)

deadlock freedom:
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock-free.

Item

i
,
subprotocol fidelity
, sets our notion of refinement apart from standard refinement.
We motivate this difference briefly using an example.
Consider the CSM consisting of the subset construction for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, depicted in
Fig.

4
.
This CSM recognizes only words of the form

({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm)^{ω}
.
It is nonetheless considered to refine the global type

\mathbf{G}_{loop}\coloneq\mu t.~{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m.~t
according to the standard notion of refinement, despite the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s messages are never received by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
This is because
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}_{loop})
, containing only infinite words, is defined in terms of an asymmetric downward closure operator
\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}
, which allows receives to be infinitely postponed.
We desire a notion of refinement that allows roles to select which runs to follow in a global type, but disallows them from selecting which words to implement among ones that follow the same run.
More formally, our notion of protocol refinement prohibits selectively implementing words that are equivalent under the indistinguishability relation
\sim
: any CSM that refines another with respect to a global type has a language that is closed under
\sim
.

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm

(a)

State machine
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})

(b)

State machine
B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}

Figure 4
:
CSM violating subprotocol fidelity with respect to
\mathbf{G}_{loop}

In the remainder of the paper, we refer to refinement with respect to
\mathbf{G}
, and omit mention of
\mathbf{G}
when clear from context.
Again using the fact that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is an implementation for
\mathbf{G}
, we say that a CSM
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines
\mathbf{G}
if it refines
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

We motivate our formulation of the
Protocol Refinement
problem by posing the following variation of
Protocol Verification
, which we call
Monolithic Protocol Refinement
:

Given an implementable global type
\mathbf{G}
and a CSM
\mathcal{A}
, does
\mathcal{A}

refine

\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
?

This variation asks for a condition,
C_{1}^{\prime}
, that satisfies the equivalence:

C_{1}^{\prime}

\Leftrightarrow

\mathcal{A}
refines
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

Clearly,
C_{1}
is still a sound candidate as equivalence of two CSMs implies bi-directional protocol refinement.
It is instructive to analyze why the completeness arguments for
C_{1}
fail.
Recall that the completeness proofs for
Send Decoration Validity
and
Receive Decoration Validity
used the violation of each condition to obtain a local state with a non-empty decoration set, which in turn gives rise to a prefix in
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
that must be a trace in the subset construction. This trace is then replayed in the arbitrary CSM, extended in the arbitrary CSM, and then replayed again in the subset construction.
This sequence of replaying arguments critically relied on both the assumption that
\mathcal{A}
refines
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, and the assumption that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines
\mathcal{A}
.

If we cannot assume that
\mathcal{A}
recognizes every behavior of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, then the reachable local states of
\mathcal{A}
are no longer precisely characterized by having a non-empty decoration set.

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}

(a)

State machine
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}

(b)

State machine
A^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}

(c)

State machine
A^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}

Figure 5
:
Subset construction for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and two state machines for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
for
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}

Consider the example global type
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
:

\small\mathbf{G}^{\prime}\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0\,\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,0\,\end{cases}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0\,\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,0\,\end{cases}\end{cases}

Let the CSM
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
consist of the subset construction automaton for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and the state machines
A^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
A^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
, given in

Figs.

5(b)
and
5(c)
.
The receive transitions highlighted in red are safe despite violating
Receive Decoration Validity
, because
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
coordinate with each other on which runs of
\mathbf{G}
they eliminate:
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
chooses to never send a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, thus
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s highlighted transition is safe, and conversely,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
never chooses to send
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
, thus
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
’s highlighted transition is safe.
Consequently,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
refines
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
despite violating
C_{1}
.

This example shows that any condition
C_{1}^{\prime}
that is compositional must sacrifice completeness. In fact, deciding whether an arbitrary CSM
\mathcal{A}
refines the subset construction
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
for some global type
\mathbf{G}
can be shown to be PSPACE-hard via a reduction from the deadlock-freedom problem for 1-safe Petri nets
[
24
]
.
We refer the reader to
Section

0.A.2
for the full construction.

Lemma 7

The
Monolithic Protocol Refinement
problem is PSPACE-hard.

Fortunately, we can recover completeness and tractability by only allowing changes to one state machine in
\mathcal{A}
at a time.
Next, we formalize the notions of
CSM contexts
and
well-behavedness
with respect to
\mathbf{G}
.
We use
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to denote a CSM context with a hole for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, and
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to denote the CSM obtained by instantiating the context with state machine
A
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
We define well-behaved contexts in terms of the canonical implementation
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.

Definition 14
(Well-behaved CSM contexts with respect to
\mathbf{G}
)

Let
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a CSM context.
We say that
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is well-behaved with respect to
\mathbf{G}
if
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines
\mathbf{G}
.
We omit
\mathbf{G}
when clear from context.

Protocol Refinement
asks to find a
C_{2}
that satisfies the following:

Theorem 5.1

Let
\mathbf{G}
be an implementable global type,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a role, and
A
,
B
be state machines for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
such that for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines
\mathbf{G}
.
Then, for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
if and only if
C_{2}
is satisfied.

5.1
Protocol Refinement

Relative to Subset Construction

As a stepping stone, we first consider the special case of
Protocol Refinement
when
B
is the subset construction automaton for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
That is, we present
C_{2}^{\prime}
that satisfies the following equivalence:

C_{2}^{\prime}

\Leftrightarrow
for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines

\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

The relaxation on language equality from
Protocol Verification
means that state machine
A
no longer needs to satisfy
Local Language Inclusion
, which grants us more flexibility: state machines are now permitted to remove send events.
Let us revisit our example global type,
\mathbf{G}_{1}
:

\small\mathbf{G}_{1}\coloneq+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0\,\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{\to}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.10994pt{:}\kern 1.10994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,0\,\end{cases}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}

(a)

Removing sends

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}

(b)

Removing receives

Figure 6
:
Two candidate implementations for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

Consider the candidate state machine
for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
given in

Fig.

6(a)
.
The CSM obtained from inserting this state machine into any well-behaved context refines
\mathbf{G}
, despite the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
never sends
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
.
In general, send events can safely be removed from reachable states in a local state machine without violating subprotocol fidelity or deadlock freedom, as long as
not all
of them are removed.

The same is not true of receive events, on the other hand.
The state machine
in

Fig.

6(b)

is not a safe candidate for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, because it causes a deadlock in the well-behaved context that consists of the subset construction for every other role.

Our characterization intuitively follows the notion that input types (receive events) are covariant, and output types (send events) are contravariant. However, note that the state machine above cannot be represented in existing works
[
26
,
8
,
20
]
: their local types support neither states with both outgoing send and receive events, nor states with outgoing send or receive events to/from different roles.

Our characterization
C_{2}^{\prime}
reuses
Send Decoration Validity
,
Receive Decoration Validity
and
Final State Validity
from
C_{1}
, but splits
Transition Exhaustivity
into a separate condition for send and receive events, to reflect the aforementioned asymmetry between them.

Definition 15
(
C_{2}^{\prime}
)

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
be a role and let
A=(Q,\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},s_{0},\delta,F)
be a state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

C_{2}^{\prime}
is satisfied when the following conditions hold in addition to
Send Decoration Validity
,
Receive Decoration Validity
and
Final State Validity
:

•

Send Preservation
: every state containing a send-originating global state must have at least one outgoing send transition:

\forall s\in Q.~\exists G\in Q_{\mathbf{G},!}.~G\in d(t)\implies\exists x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!},~s^{\prime}\in Q.~s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta
.

•

Receive Exhaustivity
: every receive transition that is enabled in some global state decorating
s
must be an outgoing transition from
s
:

\forall s\in Q.~\forall G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}.~G\in d(s)\land x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},?}\implies\exists s^{\prime}\in Q.~s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta
.

We want to show the following equivalence:

C_{2}^{\prime}

\Leftrightarrow
for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines

\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

We first prove the soundness of
C_{2}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 8
(Soundness of
C_{2}^{\prime}
)

If
C_{2}^{\prime}
holds, then for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines

\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Proof

Let
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a well-behaved context with respect to
\mathbf{G}
.
Like before, we first prove that any trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is a trace in
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Claim 1:

\forall~w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{\infty}
.
w
is a trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\implies w
is a trace in
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

The proof of Claim 1 for
C_{2}^{\prime}
differs from that for
C_{1}
in only two ways.
We discuss the differences in detail below, and avoid repeating the rest of the proof.

1.

C_{1}
grants that every role’s state machine satisfies
Send Decoration Validity
and
Receive Decoration Validity
, whereas
C_{2}
only guarantees the conditions for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Correspondingly,
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
only differs from
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s state machine; all other roles’ state machines are identical between the two CSMs.
Therefore, the induction step requires a case analysis on the role whose alphabet the event
x
belongs to.
In the case that
x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, the induction hypothesis is trivially re-established by the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s state machine is identical in both CSMs.
In the case that
x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
, we proceed to reason that
x
can also be performed by
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
in the same well-behaved context.

2.

C_{1}
includes
Transition Exhaustivity
, which allows us to conclude that given a run with unique splitting
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
matching
w
and the fact that
G\in s
, there must exist a transition

s\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}s^{\prime\prime}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s state machine.

Lemma

3
can then be instantiated directly with
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
to complete the proof.

C_{2}
, on the other hand, splits
Transition Exhaustivity
into
Send Preservation
and
Receive Exhaustivity
, and we can only establish that such a transition exists and reuse the proof in the case that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},?}
.
Since
A
is permitted to remove send events, if
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}
, the transition
s\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}s^{\prime\prime}
may not exist at all in
A
.
However, the existence of a run
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
where
l
is a send event for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
makes
G
a send-originating global state in
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s projection by erasure automaton.

Send Preservation
thus guarantees that there exists a transition
s\xrightarrow{x^{\prime}}s^{\prime\prime\prime}
in
A
such that
x^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}
.
By
Send Decoration Validity
,
x^{\prime}
originates from
G
in the projection by erasure, and we can find another run
\rho^{\prime}
such that

\alpha^{\prime}\cdot G\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}G^{\prime\prime}\cdot\beta^{\prime}
is the unique splitting for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
matching
w
and
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}=x^{\prime}
.
We satisfy the assumption that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm\notin M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G^{\prime\prime}\ldots)}
by instantiating
Receive Decoration Validity
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}
,
s\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}s^{\prime\prime}
and
G^{\prime\prime}
.
The fact that
G^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{tr-dest}(d_{\mathbf{G}}(s)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}d_{\mathbf{G}}(s^{\prime\prime}))
follows from the fact that
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}G^{\prime\prime}\cdot\beta^{\prime}
is a run in
\mathbf{G}
and
Definition

9
.
Instantiating
Lemma

3
with
\rho^{\prime}
, we obtain
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}=x
, which is a contradiction:
x
is a receive event and
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
is a send event.
Thus, it cannot be the case that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}
.

This concludes our proof that any trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is also a trace in
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

The following claim completes our soundness proof:

Claim 2:

\forall~w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
.
w
is terminated in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\implies w
is terminated in
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
w
is maximal in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

The proof of Claim 2 for
C_{1}
again relies on
Local Language Inclusion
, which is unavailable to
C_{2}^{\prime}
.
Instead, we turn to
Send Preservation
,
Receive Exhaustivity
and
Final State Validity
to establish this claim.
Let
w
be a terminated trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
By Claim 1, it holds that
w
is a trace in
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Let
\xi
be the channel configuration uniquely determined by
w
.
Let
(\vec{s},\xi)
be the
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
configuration reached on
w
, and let
(\vec{t},\xi)
be the
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
configuration reached on
w
.
To see that
w
is terminated in
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, suppose by contradiction that
w
is not terminated in
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Because
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is deadlock-free, and because the state machines for all non-
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
roles are identical between the two CSMs, it must be the case that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
witnesses the non-termination of
w
, in other words,
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
can take a transition that
A
cannot.
Let
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}
be the transition that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can take from
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Let
G
be a state in
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
; such a state is guaranteed to exist by the fact that no reachable states in the subset construction are empty.
Then, in the projection by erasure automaton, the initial state reaches
G
on
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.
By the fact that
w
is a trace of
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, it holds that
s_{0}
reaches
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
on
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
in
A
.
By the definition of state decoration,
G\in d(\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.

•

If
x\in\Sigma_{!}
, it follows that
G
is a send-originating global state.
By
Send Preservation
, for any state in
A
that contains at least one send-originating global state, of which
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is one, there exists a transition
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\xrightarrow{x^{\prime}}t^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}
.
Because send transitions in a CSM are always enabled, role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can take this transition in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
We reach a contradiction to the fact that
w
is terminated in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

•

If
x\in\Sigma_{?}
, it follows that
G
is a receive-originating global state.
From
Receive Exhaustivity
, any receive event that originates from any global state in
d(\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
must also originate from
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Therefore, there must exist
t^{\prime}
such that
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\xrightarrow{x}t^{\prime}
is a transition in
B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Because the channel configuration is identical in both CSMs, role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can take this transition in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
We again reach a contradiction to the fact that
w
is terminated in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

To see that
w
is maximal in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, observe that for all roles
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}=\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
. Thus, it remains to show that
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is a final state in
A
.
Because
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is a final state, by the definition of the subset construction there exists a global state
G\in\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
such that the projection erasure automaton reaches
G
on
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
and
G
is a final state.
Because
A
reaches
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
on
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
, by
Definition

9
it holds that
G\in d(\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.
By
Final State Validity
, it holds that
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is a final state in
A
.
This concludes our proof that any terminated trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is also a terminated trace in
\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and is maximal in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Together, Claim 1 and 2 establish that
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
satisfies language inclusion (
Item

ii
) and deadlock freedom (
Item

iii
).
It remains to show that
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
satisfies subprotocol fidelity (
Item

i
).
This follows immediately from
[
39
, Lemma 22]
, which states that all CSM languages are closed under the indistinguishability relation
\sim
.

∎

Lemma 9
(Completeness of
C_{2}^{\prime}
)

If for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines

\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, then
C_{2}^{\prime}
holds.

As before, we prove the modus tollens of this implication, which states that if
C_{2}^{\prime}
does not hold, then there exists a well-behaved context
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
such that
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
does not protocol-refine

\mathcal{A}[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

We first turn our attention to finding a well-behaved witness context
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
such that we can refute subprotocol fidelity, language inclusion, or deadlock freedom.
It turns out that the context consisting of the subset construction automaton for every other role is a suitable witness. We denote this context by
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and note that it is trivially well-behaved because
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

Recall from the completeness arguments for
C_{1}
that we obtained a violating state in some state machine
A
with a non-empty decoration set from the negation of each condition in
C_{1}
.
From this state’s decoration set we obtained a witness global state
G
, and in turn a run
\alpha\cdot G
in
\mathbf{G}
, and from the assumption that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines
\mathcal{A}
, we argued that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G))
is a trace in
\mathcal{A}
.
We then showed that
A
is in the violating state in the
\mathcal{A}
configuration reached on
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G))
, and from there we used each violated condition to find a contradiction.

The completeness proof for
C_{2}^{\prime}
cannot similarly use the fact that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Instead, we must separately establish that every state with a non-empty decoration set can be reached on a trace shared by both
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
The following lemma achieves this:

Lemma 10

Let
A
be a state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
s
be a state in
A
.
Let
G\in d(s)
, and let
u\in\Sigma^{*}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a word such that

s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s
in
A
.
Then, there exists a run
\alpha\cdot G
of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
such that

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G)){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=u
,

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G))
is a trace in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and in the CSM configuration reached on
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G))
,
A
is in state
s
.

With
Lemma

10
replacing the assumption that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, we can reuse the construction in
Lemma

6
to obtain a word that is a trace in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
but not a trace in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, thus evidencing the necessity of
Send Decoration Validity
and
Receive Decoration Validity
. The proof of
Lemma

11
proceeds identically to that of
Lemma

6
and is thus omitted.

Lemma 11

If
A
violates
Send Decoration Validity
or
Receive Decoration Validity
, then it does not hold that for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

We also use
Lemma

10
to show the necessity of
Send Preservation
,
Receive Exhaustivity
and
Final State Validity
.
As a starting point, let
A
,
s
,
u
and
\alpha\cdot G
be obtained from
Lemma

10
and the violation of
Send Preservation
.
To show the necessity of
Send Preservation
, we consider the largest extension
v
of
u
in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
In the case that
u
is terminated in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, we refute deadlock freedom from the fact that
u
is not maximal:
G\in s
is a send-originating state, and final states in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
do not contain outgoing transitions.
If
v\neq u
, there exists a run
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
such that

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}=v{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.
By subprotocol fidelity,
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta))
is a trace in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Consequently,
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta)){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
is a prefix in
A
.
We find a contradiction from the fact that
A
is deterministic and there is no outgoing transition labeled
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm
from
s
.
Similar arguments can be used to show the necessity of
Receive Exhaustivity
.
Finally, for
Final State Validity
, in the case that
s
is non-final in
A
but contains a final state in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
, we can instantiate
Lemma

10
with this final state and show that
u
evidences a deadlock.

Lemma 12

If
A
violates
Send Preservation
,
Receive Exhaustivity
or
Final State Validity
, then it does not hold that for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

5.2
Protocol Refinement

(General Case)

Equipped with the solution to a special case, we are ready to revisit the general case of
Protocol Refinement
, which asks to find a
C_{2}
that satisfies the following:

C_{2}

\Leftrightarrow
for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines

\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Critical to the former problems is the fact that the state decoration function precisely captures those states in a local state machine that are reachable in some CSM execution, under some assumptions on the context: a state is reachable if and only if its decoration set is non-empty.
This allows the conditions in
C_{1}
and
C_{2}^{\prime}
to precisely characterize the reachable local states.

The second problem generalizes the subset projection to an arbitrary state machine
B
, and asks whether a candidate state machine
A
(the subtype) refines
B
(the supertype) in any well-behaved context.
Unfortunately, we cannot simply decorate the subtype with the supertype’s states, because not all states in the supertype are reachable.
Instead, we need to restrict the set of states in the supertype to those that themselves have non-empty decoration sets with respect to
\mathbf{G}
.

In the remainder of this section, let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
be a role, let
B=(Q_{B},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},t_{0},\delta_{B},F_{B})
denote the supertype state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and let
A=(Q_{A},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},s_{0},\delta_{A},F_{A})
denote the subtype state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
We modify our state decoration function in
Definition

9
to map states of
A
to subsets of states in
B
that themselves have non-empty decoration sets with respect to
\mathbf{G}
.

Definition 16
(State decoration with respect to a supertype)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
be a role, and let
B=(Q_{B},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},t_{0},\delta_{B},F_{B})
and
A=(Q_{A},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},s_{0},\delta_{A},F_{A})
be two deterministic finite state machines for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
We define a total function
d_{\mathbf{G},B,A}:Q^{\prime}\rightarrow 2^{Q}
that maps each state in
A
to a subset of states in
B
such that:

d_{\mathbf{G},B,A}(s)=\{t\in Q_{B}\mid\exists u\in\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}^{*}.~s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s\in\delta_{A}\land t_{0}\xrightarrow{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}t\in\delta_{B}\land d(t)\neq\emptyset\}

We again omit the subscripts
\mathbf{G}
and
A
when clear from context, but retain the subscript
B
to distinguish
d_{B}
from
d
in
Definition

9
.

We likewise require a generalization of
\operatorname{tr-orig}
and
\operatorname{tr-dest}
to be defined in terms of
B
, instead of the projection by erasure automaton for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Definition 17
(Transition origin and destination with respect to a supertype)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type, and let
B=(Q_{B},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},t_{0},\delta_{B},F_{B})
be a state machine.
For
x\in\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
s,s^{\prime}\subseteq Q_{B}
,
we define the set of
transition origins

\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})
and
transition destinations

\operatorname{tr-dest}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})
as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr-orig}_{B}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\{t\in s\mid\exists t^{\prime}\in s^{\prime}.\,t\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}t^{\prime}\in\delta_{B}\}\;\text{ and }

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr-dest}_{B}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\{t^{\prime}\in s^{\prime}\mid\exists t\in s.\,t\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}t^{\prime}\in\delta_{B}\}\kern 5.0pt.

We present
C_{2}
in terms of the newly defined decoration function
d_{B}
.

Definition 18
(
C_{2}
)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
be a role, and
B=(Q_{B},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},t_{0},\delta_{B},F_{B})
and
A=(Q_{A},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},s_{0},\delta_{A},F_{A})
be two deterministic state machines for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

C_{2}
is the conjunction of the following conditions:

•

Send Decoration Subtype Validity
: every send transition
s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{A}
must be enabled in all states of
B
decorating
s
:

\forall s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556ptm}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{A}.~\operatorname{tr-orig}_{B}(d_{B}(s)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556ptm}d_{B}(s^{\prime}))=d_{B}(s)
.

•

Receive Decoration Subtype Validity
: no receive transition is enabled in an alternative continuation originating from the same state:

\begin{array}[]{l}\forall s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556ptm_{1}}s_{1},~s\xrightarrow{x}s_{2}\in\delta_{A}.~x\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002pt\_\implies\\
\qquad\forall G\in\underset{t\in d_{B}(s_{2})}{\bigcup}\{d(t)\mid t\in\operatorname{tr-dest}_{B}(d_{B}(s)\xrightarrow{x}d_{B}(s_{2}))\}.~{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm_{1}\notin M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G\ldots)}.\end{array}

•

Send Subtype Preservation
: every state decorated by a send-originating global state must have at least one outgoing send transition:

\forall s\in Q_{A}.~(\underset{t\in d_{B}(s)}{\bigcup}d(t)\cap Q_{\mathbf{G},!}\neq\emptyset\,)\implies\exists x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!},\,s^{\prime}\in Q_{A}.~s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{A}
.

•

Receive Subtype Exhaustivity
: every receive transition that is enabled in some global state decorating
s
must be an outgoing transition from
s
:

\forall s\in Q_{A}.~\forall G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}.~G\in\underset{t\in d_{B}(s)}{\bigcup}d(t)\implies\exists s^{\prime}\in Q_{A}.~s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{A}
.

•

Final State Validity
: a reachable state is final if its decorating set contains a final global state:

\forall s\in Q_{A}.~\underset{t\in d_{B}(s)}{\bigcup}d(t)\neq\emptyset\implies(\underset{t\in d_{B}(s)}{\bigcup}d(t)\cap F_{\mathbf{G}}\neq\emptyset\,)\implies s\in F_{A}
.

We want to show the following equivalence to prove
Theorem

5.1
:

C_{2}

\Leftrightarrow
for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Lemma 13
(Soundness of
C_{2}
)

If
C_{2}
holds, then for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines

\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Predictably, the proof of soundness is directly adapted from the proof for
C_{2}^{\prime}
by applying suitable “liftings”, and can be found in
Section

0.A.2
.

Lemma 14
(Completeness of
C_{2}
)

If for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines

\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, then
C_{2}
holds.

Again, we prove the modus tollens of this implication, and we again are required to find a witness well-behaved context
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, such that
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
does not refine
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
under the assumption of the negation of
C_{2}
.
In the special case where
B
is the subset construction automaton, we observed that any state in
A
with a non-empty decoration set with respect to
\mathbf{G}
is reachable by the CSM consisting of
A
and the subset construction context, denoted
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
We were therefore able to use
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
as the witness well-behaved context.
A similar characterization is true in the general case: a state in
A
is reachable by
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
if it has a non-empty decoration set with respect to
B
.
This in turn depends on the fact that we only label states in
A
with states in
B
that themselves have non-empty decorating sets with respect to
\mathbf{G}
.
The following lemma lifts
Lemma

10
to the general problem setting:

Lemma 15

Let
A,B
be two state machines for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, such that for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines
\mathbf{G}
.
Let
s
be a state in
A
, and let
t
be a state in
B
such that
t\in d_{B}(s)
.
Let
u\in\Sigma^{*}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a word such that

s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s
in
A
.
Then, there exists a run
\alpha\cdot G
of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
such that

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G)){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=u
,

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G))
is a trace in both
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and in the CSM configuration reached on
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G))
,
A
is in state
s
.

Proof

From the fact that
t\in d_{B}(s)
and the definition of state decoration (
Definition

16
), it holds that

d(t)\neq\emptyset
and

t_{0}\xrightarrow{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}t\in\delta_{B}
.
Let
G\in d(t)
.
We apply
Lemma

10
to obtain a run
\alpha\cdot G
such that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G)){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=u
,
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G))
is a trace in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and in the
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
configuration reached on
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G))
,
B
is in state
t
.
Because
s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s\in\delta_{A}
, and all non-
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
state machines are identical from
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, it is clear that

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G))
is also a trace of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and in the CSM configuration reached on
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\alpha\cdot G))
,
A
is in state
s
.

∎

Having found our witness well-behaved context
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, established
Lemma

15
to replace
Lemma

10
, and observed that the violation of each condition in
C_{2}
likewise yields a state with a non-empty decoration set with respect to
B
, completeness then amounts to showing the existence of a
w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
such that
w
refutes subprotocol fidelity, language inclusion, or deadlock freedom.
Recall that the proofs for the necessity of
Send Preservation
,
Receive Exhaustivity
and
Final State Validity
in the case where
B
is the subset construction constructed a trace that refuted either subprotocol fidelity or deadlock freedom.
These two properties are identical across both formulations of the problem, and therefore the construction can be wholly reused to show the necessity of
Send Subtype Preservation
,
Receive Subtype Exhaustivity
and
Final State Subtype Validity
.

Lemma 16

If
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
violates
Send Decoration Subtype Validity
or
Receive Decoration Subtype Validity
, then it does not hold that for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

The proofs for the necessity of
Send Decoration Validity
and
Receive Decoration Validity
, on the other hand, construct a word that is a trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
but not a trace in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G})[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
In the general case, we can show that the same construction is a trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
but not a trace in
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
We omit the proofs to avoid redundancy.

Lemma 17

If
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
violates
Send Subtype Preservation
,
Receive Subtype Exhaustivity
, or
Final State Subtype Validity
, then it does not hold that for all well-behaved contexts
\mathcal{A}[\cdot]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

6
Complexity Analysis

We complete our discussion with a complexity analysis of the two considered problems, building on the characterizations established in
Theorem

4.1
and
Theorem

5.1
.

For the
Protocol Verification
problem, let
m
be the size of
\mathcal{A}
and
n
the size of
\mathbf{G}
. Moreover, let
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be the local implementation of some role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
\mathcal{A}
. Observe that the sets
d_{\mathbf{G}}(s)
for each state
s
of
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
as well as the sets
M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G^{\prime}\ldots)}
for each subterm
G^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{G}
are at most of size
n
. It is then easy to see that
C_{1}
can be checked in time polynomial in
n
and
m
, provided that the sets
d_{\mathbf{G}}(s)
and
M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G^{\prime}\ldots)}
are also computable in polynomial time.

To see this for the sets
M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G^{\prime}\ldots)}
, observe that the definition expands each occurrence of a recursion variable in
\mathbf{G}
at most once. So the traversal takes time
O(n^{2})
. For each traversed event
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{\to}\kern 1.19995pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.19995pt{:}\kern 1.19995ptm
in
\mathbf{G}
, we need to perform a constant number of lookup, insertion, and deletion operations on a set of size at most
n
, which takes time
O(\log n)
. The time for computing
M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G^{\prime}\ldots)}
is thus in
O(n^{2}\log n)
.

Similarly, observe that the function
d_{\mathbf{G}}
can be computed for the local implementation of each role
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
using a simple fixpoint loop. Each set
d_{\mathbf{G}}(s)
can be represented as a bit vector of size
n
, making all set operations constant time. The loop inserts at most
n
subterms of
\mathbf{G}
into each
d_{\mathbf{G}}(s)
, which takes time
O(mn)
for all insertions. Moreover, for each
G
inserted into a set
d_{\mathbf{G}}(s)
and each transition
s\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}}s^{\prime}
in
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, we need to compute the set
\{G^{\prime}\mid G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}\}
which is then added to
d_{\mathbf{G}}(s^{\prime})
. Computing these sets takes time
O(mn)
for each
G
and
s
.

Following analogous reasoning, we can also establish that
C_{2}
is checkable in polynomial time.

Theorem 6.1

The
Protocol Verification
and
Protocol Refinement
problems are decidable in polynomial time.

7
Related Work

Session types were first introduced in binary form by Honda in 1993
[
29
]
.
Binary session types describe interactions between two participants, and communication safety of binary sessions amounts to channel duality.
Binary session types were generalized to multiparty session types – describing interactions between more than two participants – by Honda, Yoshida and Carbone in 2008
[
31
]
, and the corresponding notion of safety was generalized from duality to multiparty consistency.
Binary session types were inspired by and enjoy a close connection to linear logic
[
28
,
49
,
11
]
.
Horne generalizes this connection to multiparty session types and non-commutative extensions of linear logic
[
32
]
. The connection between multiparty session types and logic is also explored in
[
12
,
10
,
13
]
.
MSTs have since been extensively studied and widely adopted in practical programming languages; we refer the reader to
[
19
]
for a comprehensive survey.

Session type syntax.

Session type frameworks have enjoyed various extensions since their inception.
In particular, the choice operator for both global and local types has received considerable attention over the years.
MSTs were originally introduced as global types, with a
directed
choice operator that restricted a sender to sending different messages to the same recipient.
[
15
]
and
[
39
]
relax this restriction to
sender-driven choice
, which allows a sender to send different messages to different recipients, and increases the expressivity of global types. Our paper targets global types with sender-driven choice.
For local types, a direct comparison can be drawn to the
\pi
-calculus, for which
mixed choice
was shown to be strictly more expressive than
separate choice

[
42
]
.
Mixed choices allow both send and receive actions, whereas separate choices consist purely of either sends or receives.

[
37
]
showed that any global type with sender-driven choice can be implemented by a CSM with only separate choice.
Mixed choice for binary local types was investigated in
[
14
]
, although
[
43
]
later showed that this variant falls short of the full expressive power of mixed choice
\pi
-calculus, and instead can only express separate choice
\pi
-calculus.
Other communication primitives have also been studied, such as channel delegation
[
31
,
30
,
17
]
, dependent predicates
[
47
,
48
]
, parametrization
[
22
,
18
]
and data refinement
[
50
]
.

Session type semantics.

MSTs were introduced in
[
31
]
with a process algebra semantics. The connection to CSMs was established in
[
21
]
, which defines a class of CSMs whose state machines can be represented as local types, called
Communicating Session Automata
(CSA). CSAs inherit from the local types they represent restrictions on choice discussed above, “tree-like” restrictions on the structure (see
[
46
]
for a characterization), and restrictions on outgoing transitions from final states. The CSM implementation model in our work assumes none of the above restrictions, and is thus true to its name.

Session subtyping.

Session subtyping was first introduced by
[
25
]
in the context of the
\pi
-calculus, which was in turn inspired by Pierce and Sangiorgi’s work on subtyping for channel endpoints
[
44
]
.
The session types literature distinguishes between two notions of subtyping based on the network assumptions of the framework:
synchronous
and
asynchronous subtyping
.
Both notions respect Liskov and Wing’s substitution principle
[
38
]
, but differ in the guarantees provided.
We discuss each in turn.

Synchronous subtyping follows the notions of covariance and contravariance introduced by
[
25
]
, and checks that a subtype contains fewer sends and more receives than its supertype.
For binary synchronous session types, Lange and Yoshida
[
34
]
show that subtyping can be decided in quadratic time via model checking of a characteristic formulae in the modal
\mu
-calculus.
For multiparty synchronous session types, Ghilezan et al.
[
26
]
present a precise subtyping relation that is universally quantified over all contexts, and restricts the local type syntax to directed choice.
As mentioned in
Section

1
,
[
26
]
, their subtyping relation is incomplete when generalized to asynchronous multiparty sessions with directed choice.
As discussed in
Section

2
, their subtyping relation is further incomplete when generalized to asynchronous multiparty sessions with mixed choice, due to the “peculiarity […] that, apart from a pair of inactive session types, only inputs and outputs from/to a same participant can be related”
[
26
]
.
The complexity of the subtyping relation in
[
26
]
is not mentioned.

Unlike subtyping relations for synchronous sessions which preserve language inclusion, subtyping relations for asynchronous sessions instead focus on deadlock-free optimizations that permute roles’ local order of send and receive actions, also called
asynchronous message reordering
, or AMR
[
20
]
.
First proposed for binary sessions by Mostrous and Yoshida
[
40
]
, and for multiparty sessions by Mostrous et al.
[
41
]
, this notion of subtyping does not satisfy subprotocol fidelity in general; indeed, in some cases, the set of behaviors recognized by a supertype is entirely disjoint from that of its subtype
[
5
]
.
Asynchronous subtyping was shown to be undecidable for both binary and multiparty session types
[
35
,
