6
]
.
Existing works are thus either restricted to binary protocols
[
35
,
5
,
6
,
1
]
, prohibit non-deterministic choice involving multiple receivers
[
27
,
7
]
, or make strong fairness assumptions on the network
[
7
]
.

The connection between session subtyping and behavioral contract refinement has been studied only in the context of binary session types, and is thus out of scope of our work. We refer the reader to
[
26
]
for a survey.

Appendix 0.A
Appendix

We define a family of

.

Let
w,w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}
be sequences of events s.t.
w\sim_{1}w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}\sim_{i}w^{\prime\prime}
for some
i
.
Then,
w\sim_{i+1}w^{\prime\prime}
.
We define
w\sim u
if
w\sim_{n}u
for some
n
.

It is easy to see that
\sim
is an equivalence relation.
Define
u\preceq_{\sim}v
if there is
w\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
u.w\sim v
.
Observe that
u\sim v
iff

u\preceq_{\sim}v
and
v\preceq_{\sim}u
.

For infinite words
u,v\in\Sigma^{\omega}
, we define
u\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}v

if for each finite prefix
u^{\prime}
of
u
, there is a finite prefix
v^{\prime}
of
v
such that

u^{\prime}\preceq_{\sim}v^{\prime}
.
Define
u\sim v
iff
u\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}v
and
v\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}u
.

We lift the equivalence relation
\sim
on
\Sigma^{\infty}
to languages:

\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(L)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{w^{\prime}\mid\bigvee\begin{array}[]{l}w^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*}\land∃w∈\Sigma^{*}.\;w\in L\text{ and }w^{\prime}\sim w\\
w^{\prime}∈\Sigma^{ω}\land\exists w\in\Sigma^{\omega}.\;w\in L\text{ and }w^{\prime}\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}w\end{array}}}\right\}

For the infinite case, we take the downward closure w.r.t.
\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}
.
Notice that the closure operator is asymmetric.
Consider the protocol
({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm)^{ω}
.
Since we do not make any fairness assumption on scheduling, we need to include in the closure the execution where only the sender is scheduled, i.e.,

({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm)^{ω}\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm)^{ω}\kern 5.0pt.

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}

l
is of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\xrightarrow{\_}:\_
, with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
sending a message. We prove a contradiction in each case.

First, we establish a claim that is used in both cases, and relies only on the fact that
\rho
is consistent with
w
and
wx
is a trace of
\mathcal{A}
.

Let
\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
denote the largest consistent prefix of
\rho
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
; it is clear that

\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}=\alpha\cdot G
. Formally,

\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}=max\{\rho^{\prime}~|~\rho^{\prime}\leq\rho~\land~\bigl(\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\rho^{\prime}))\bigr){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}\leq w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
be defined analogously.

Claim:

\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}<\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
Intuitively,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is ahead of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
in
\rho
due to the half-duplex property of CSMs and the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
is the sender.
Formally,
[
39
, Lemma 19]
implies
\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})=u
where

\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556pt\_})=\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556pt\_}).u
.
Because
\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
contains at least
m
by assumption,

|\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556pt\_})|>|\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556pt\_})|
.
Because

\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556pt\_})<\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556pt\_})

and traces of CSMs are channel-compliant
[
39
, Lemma 19]
, it holds that

\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
contains all

\lvert\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556pt\_})\rvert

transition labels of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:\_

that are contained in
\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
,
plus at least one more of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m
.
Because both
\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
are prefixes of
\rho
,
it must be the case that

\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}<\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
This concludes the proof of the above claim.

Case:

l={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime}\neq m
.
We discharge this case by showing a contradiction to the fact that
m
is at the head of the channel between
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.

Because
\alpha\cdot G\leq\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}<\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
from the claim above, it must be the case that

\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\leq\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}

and

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm^{\prime}
is in
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}}
.
From
[
39
, Lemma 19]
, it follows that

\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556pt\_})=\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.0653pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.65556pt\_}).m^{\prime}.u^{\prime}
and

\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})=m^{\prime}.u^{\prime}
, i.e.
m^{\prime}
is at the head of the channel between
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
We reach a contradiction.

Case:

\forall m^{\prime}.~l\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m^{\prime}
.
It follows that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm^{\prime}
for any
m^{\prime}
.
We discharge this case by showing that

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm\in M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}_{(G^{\prime}\ldots)}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\leq\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
Then, there exists a transition labeled
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m
that occurs in the suffix
G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
.
Let

G_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m}G_{0}^{\prime}

be the earliest occurrence of such a transition in the suffix, then:

\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}=\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\ldots G_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m}G_{0}^{\prime}\dots\kern 5.0pt.

Note that
G_{0}
must be a syntactic subterm of
G^{\prime}
.
In order for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm\in M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}_{(G^{\prime}\ldots)}
to hold, it suffices to show that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\notin\mathcal{B}
in the recursive call to
M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G^{\prime}\dots)}
.
We argue this from the definition of
M
and the fact that
\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}=\alpha\cdot G
.
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{B}
.
Because
M
only adds receivers of already blocked senders to
\mathcal{B}
and
M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}_{(G^{\prime}\ldots)}
starts with
\mathcal{B}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\}
, there must exist a chain of message exchanges
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i+1}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}:m_{i}
in
G^{\prime}
with
1\leq i<n
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{n}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{1}
. That is,
G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
must be of the form

G^{\prime}\dots G_{n-1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{n-1}:m_{n-1}}G_{n-1}^{\prime}\ldots G_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{2}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}:m_{1}}G_{1}^{\prime}\ldots G_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m}G_{0}^{\prime}\ldots\kern 5.0pt.

Let
m_{0}=m
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{0}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
. We show by induction over
i
that for all
i\in[1,n]

\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\dots G_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i-1}:m_{i-1}}G_{i}^{\prime}~\leq~\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}}\kern 5.0pt.

We then obtain the desired contradiction with the fact that
\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{n}}=\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}=\alpha\cdot G^{\prime}
.
The base case of the induction follows immediately from the construction.
For the induction step, assume that

\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\dots G_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i-1}:m_{i-1}}G_{i}^{\prime}~\leq~\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}}\kern 5.0pt.

From the definition of
\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}}
and the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}
is the active role in
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i+1}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm_{i}
, it follows that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i+1}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm_{i}\in w
. Hence, we must also have
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i+1}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm_{i}\in w
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i+1}
is the active role in
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i+1}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm_{i}
, we can conclude

\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\dots G_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i+1}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i}:m_{i}}G_{i+1}^{\prime}~\leq~\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}_{i+1}}\kern 5.0pt.

This concludes the proof of
Lemma

3
.

∎

See
5

Proof

From the negation of
Transition Exhaustivity
, we find a witness trace
v
such that
v
is a trace in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
but not a trace in
\mathcal{A}
, thus contradicting the fact that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a role that violates
Transition Exhaustivity
.
Let
s
be a state such that there exists
G\in d_{\mathbf{G}}(s)
with
G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}
but no transition outgoing from
s
labeled with
x
.
By the definition of state decoration, there exists
u\in\Sigma^{*}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
such that
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
reaches
s
on
u
from its initial state, and the projection by erasure automaton for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
reaches
G
on
u
from its initial state.
Because
G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}
,
it holds that
q_{0,\mathbf{G}}\xrightarrow{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}
is a run in the projection by erasure automaton for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Let
\rho
denote this run, and let
w=\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\texttt{{trace}}(\rho))
.
Then, it holds that
ux\leq w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.
Because
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
implements
\mathbf{G}
,
w
is a trace of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
Consequently,
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
is a prefix of
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Because

is a prefix of

,

A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Because

on

.
However, there does not exist an outgoing transition labeled with

from

is a prefix of
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

From the negation of
Final State Validity
, we find a witness trace

that is maximally terminated in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

, thus contradicting the fact that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a role that violates

.
Let

be a state such that there exists

with

but
s\notin F_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Let

such that
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
reaches

Because
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
refines

Because

on

In other words, in the

configuration reached on

We reach a contradiction.

∎

Proof

We show the PSPACE-hardness of the monolithic refinement problem by a reduction from the PSPACE-hard problem of deciding deadlock freedom for 1-safe Petri nets
[
24
]
.
Let
(N,M_{0})
be a 1-safe Petri net, with
N=(S,T,F)
.

We construct a CSM
\mathcal{A}_{N}
and a global type
\mathbf{G}_{N}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{N}
refines
\mathbf{G}_{N}
if and only if the Petri net is deadlock-free.

We first describe the construction of
\mathcal{A}_{N}
.

\mathcal{A}_{N}
consists of one state machine per place in
S
, one state machine per transition in
T
, and one special coordinator role, which we denote
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Each place state machine tracks whether its place is marked by
0
or
1
, and responds to messages to increment or decrement its marking.
Each transition state machine communicates with its input and output place state machines to check whether its transition is enabled, and to update place markings.
The coordinator
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
first asks each transition state machine whether its transition is enabled.
This querying can be performed in an arbitrary fixed order on
T
.
If at least one transition is enabled,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
then non-deterministically picks a transition to fire.
Depending on whether the picked transition is enabled, the input and output place state machines update the configuration, and the transition state machine returns the control flow to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, which repeats this process with the new configuration.
If no transition is enabled,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
enters a sink state with no outgoing transitions, thus causing a deadlock in
\mathcal{A}_{N}
.

Each message exchange between roles is echoed with an acknowledgement, and the CSM thus constructed is 1-bounded: there is at most one message in flight at any point during its execution.
Intuitively,
\mathcal{A}_{N}
simulates the firing of transitions in the Petri nets via message exchanges, and represents all valid execution traces of the Petri net as CSM traces.

Correspondingly, we construct a global type
\mathbf{G}_{N}
whose language includes not only all execution traces of
\mathcal{A}_{N}
, but also traces that do not correspond to valid execution traces in the Petri net.

\mathbf{G}_{N}
achieves this by mimicing the control flow of the
\mathcal{A}_{N}
, but decoupling the message contents from the underlying Petri net configuration: at each control flow point, roles non-deterministically choose a message to send.

If the Petri net is deadlock-free, then
\mathcal{A}_{N}
is also deadlock-free and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{N})
includes only infinite words: because each configuration has at least one enabled transition,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s sink state will never be reached.
Because
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{N})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}_{N})
by construction, it holds that
\mathcal{A}_{N}
refines
\mathbf{G}_{N}
.
On the contrary, if
\mathcal{A}_{N}
refines
\mathbf{G}_{N}
and is thus deadlock-free, then the Petri net is also deadlock-free, as
\mathcal{A}_{N}
can simulate all valid execution traces of the Petri net.

See
13

Proof

First, we prove that any trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is a trace in
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
:

Claim 1:

\forall~w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
.
w
is a trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

w
is a trace in
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

We prove the claim by induction on

.
The base case, where

, is trivially discharged by the fact that

In the inductive step, assume that

is a trace of
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

is a trace of

\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

w
is also a trace of
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

, and let

,

for

To show that

is a trace of
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

and a transition

By the definition of state decoration (

16
), it follows that
t\in d_{B}(s)
.
Because
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines
\mathbf{G}
and is deadlock-free, it holds that all traces of
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

In other words,
w\in\text{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
.
Let

; such a run must exist from

[

.
Let

for

.
From
Definition

9
, it holds that
G\in d(t)
.

We proceed by case analysis on whether
x
is a send or receive event.

•

Case
x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}
.
Let
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm
.
By assumption, there exists
s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556ptm}s^{\prime}
in
\delta_{A}
.
We instantiate
Send Decoration Subtype Validity
from
C_{2}
with this transition to obtain:

\operatorname{tr-orig}_{B}(d_{B}(s)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 1.0653pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.65556pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.65556pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.65556ptm}d_{B}(s^{\prime}))=d_{B}(s)\kern 5.0pt.

From
t\in d_{B}(s)
, it follows immediately that there exists
t^{\prime}
such that
t\xrightarrow{x}t^{\prime}
is a transition in
B
.

•

Case
x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},?}
.
Let
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\triangleleft}\kern 1.30005pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{?}}\kern 0.80002ptm
.

We proceed by case analysis on
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.
In the case that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},?}
, from
Lemma

12
there exists a transition
t\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}t^{\prime}
in
\delta_{B}
, and from
Receive Subtype Exhaustivity
there exists a transition
s\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}s^{\prime\prime}
in
\delta_{A}
.
We can apply
Lemma

3
with
\rho
to conclude that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}=x
: we satisfy the assumption that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm\notin M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G^{\prime}\ldots)}
by instantiating
Receive Decoration Subtype Validity
with
s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}
,
s\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}}s^{\prime\prime}
, and
G^{\prime}
.
The fact that
t^{\prime}\in\operatorname{tr-dest}_{B}(d_{B}(s)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}d_{B}(s^{\prime\prime}))
follows from the existence of
t\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}t^{\prime}
in
\delta_{B}
and the definition of state decoration (
Definition

16
).
The fact that
G^{\prime}\in\operatorname{tr-dest}_{B}(d(t)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}d(t^{\prime}))
follows from the fact that
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
is a run in
\mathbf{G}
and
Definition

9
.

In the case that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}
, we again prove a contradiction.
Because
G
is a send-originating global state,
Send Subtype Preservation
guarantees that there exists a transition
s\xrightarrow{x^{\prime}}s^{\prime\prime}
in
A
such that
x^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}
.
By
Send Decoration Validity
,
x^{\prime}
originates from
G
in the projection by erasure, and we can find another run
\rho^{\prime}
such that

\alpha^{\prime}\cdot G\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}G^{\prime\prime}\cdot\beta^{\prime}
is the unique splitting for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
matching
w
, and
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}=x^{\prime}
.

We can instantiate
Lemma

3
with
\rho^{\prime}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 1.30005pt{\triangleright}\kern 0.80002pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.80002pt{\operatorname{!}}\kern 0.80002ptm\notin M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G^{\prime\prime}\ldots)}
as above to obtain
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}=x
, which is a contradiction:
x
is a receive event and
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
is a send event.

This concludes our proof of Claim 1.

Next, we show that any trace that terminates in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
also terminates in
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and is maximal in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Claim 2:

\forall~w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
.
w
is terminated in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

\implies w
is terminated in
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
w
is maximal in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Let
w
be a terminated trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Let
\xi
be the channel configuration uniquely determined by
w
.
Let
(\vec{s},\xi)
be the
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
configuration reached on
w
, and let
(\vec{t},\xi)
be the
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
configuration reached on
w
.
Let
s
,
t
denote
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
First suppose by contradiction that
w
is not terminated in
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Because the state machines for all non-
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
roles are identical between the two CSMs, and because
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is deadlock-free by assumption, it must be the case that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
witnesses the non-termination of
w
, in other words,
B
can perform an action that
A
cannot.
Let
x
be the action that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can perform from
t
.
Let
G
be a state in
d(t)
, such a state is guaranteed to exist by Claim 1 and the fact that no reachable states in
B
have empty decorating sets.
Then,
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
reaches
G
from the initial state in the projection by erasure automaton.
By the fact that
w
is a trace of
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, it holds that there exists a run with trace
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
in
A
.
By the definition of state decoration,
t\in d_{B}(s)
.

•

If
x\in\Sigma_{!}
, it follows that
G
is a send-originating global state.
By
Send Subtype Preservation
, for any state in
A
that is decorated by a state in
B
that itself is decorated by at least one send-originating global state, of which
t
is one, there exists a transition
s\xrightarrow{x^{\prime}}s^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}
.
Because send transitions in a CSM are always enabled, role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can take this transition in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
We reach a contradiction to the fact that
w
is terminated in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

•

If
x\in\Sigma_{?}
, it follows that
G
is a receive-originating global state.
From
Receive Subtype Exhaustivity
, any receive action that originates from any global state in
d(t)
for any state
t\in d_{B}(s)
must also originate from
s
.
Therefore, there must exist
s^{\prime}
such that
s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}
is a transition in
A
.
Thus, role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can take this transition in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
We again reach a contradiction to the fact that
w
is terminated in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

To see that every terminated trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in maximal, from the above we know that
w
is terminated in
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
. From the fact that
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is deadlock-free,
w
is maximal in
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
: all states in
\vec{t}
are final and all channels in
\xi
are empty.
Because
t
is a final state, by that fact that
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
refines
\mathbf{G}
there exists a global state
G\in t
such that the projection erasure automaton reaches
G
on
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
and
G
is a final state.
Because
A
reaches
s
on
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
, by the definitions of state decorations (
Definitions

9
and
16
), it holds that
G\in\underset{t\in d_{B}(s)}{\bigcup}d(t)
.
By
Final State Validity
, it holds that
s
is a final state in
A
.
This concludes our proof that any terminated trace in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is also a terminated trace in
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and is maximal in
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Together, Claim 1 and 2 establish that
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
satisfies language inclusion with respect to
\mathcal{A}[B]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
(
Item

ii
), and deadlock freedom (
Item

iii
).
It remains to show that
\mathcal{A}[A]_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
also satisfies subprotocol fidelity (
Item

i
).
This follows immediately from
[
39
, Lemma 22]
, which states that all CSM languages are closed under
\sim
.

∎
